54:CDS-B | 532:Page | 553:3
54:Common Data Set 2025-2026
76:• | 89:New guidance from IPEDS for reporting aggregate data:
76:Racial/ethnic designations are requested only for United States citizens, residents, and other eligible non-citizens.
76:Eligible non-citizens include all students who completed high school or a GED equivalency within the United States (including DACA and undocumented students)
76:and who were not on an F-1 non-immigrant student visa at the time of high school graduation.
76:More information about other eligible (for financial aid purposes) non-citizens is available at:
76:https://studentaid.gov/understandaid/eligibility/requirements/non-us-citizens.
76:Nonresident - A person who is not a citizen or national of the United States and who is in this country on a student visa
76:or temporary
76:basis and does not have the right to remain indefinitely. Do not include DACA, undocumented, or othereligible noncitizens in this category.
76:NOTE - Nonresidents are to be reported separately, in the boxes provided, rather than included in any of the
76:seven racial/ethnic categories or in race/ethnicity unknown.

76:outside of a dual enrollment program, you may report the unduplicated count as part of the non-degree-seeking students included in the
76:“Total Undergraduates (both degree & non-degree-seeking)” section.

260:H. FINANCIAL AID
78:Please refer to the following financial aid definitions when completing Section H.
78:Awarded aid: The dollar amounts offered to financial aid applicants.
78:Financial aid applicant: Any applicant who submits any one of the institutionally required financial aid applications/forms, such as the FAFSA.
78:Indebtedness: Aggregate dollar amount borrowed through any loan program (federal, state, subsidized, unsubsidized, private, etc.; excluding
78:parent loans) while the student was enrolled at an institution. Student loans co-signed by a parent are assumed to be the responsibility of the
78:student and should be included.
78:Institutional scholarships and grants: Endowed scholarships, annual gifts and tuition funded grants for which the institution determines the
78:recipient.
78:Financial need: As determined by your institution using the federal methodology and/or your institution's own standards.
78:Need-based aid: College-funded or college-administered award from institutional, state, federal, or other sources for which a student must have
78:financial need to qualify. This includes both institutional and non-institutional student aid (grants, jobs, and loans).
78:Need-based scholarship or grant aid: Scholarships and grants from institutional, state, federal, or other sources for which a student must have
78:financial need to qualify.
78:Need-based self-help aid: Loans and jobs from institutional, state, federal, or other sources for which a student must demonstrate financial need
78:to qualify.
78:Non-need-based scholarship or grant aid: Scholarships and grants, gifts, or merit-based aid from institutional, state, federal, or other sources
78:(including unrestricted funds or gifts and endowment income) awarded solely on the basis of academic achievement, merit, or any other non-
78:need-based reason. When reporting questions H1 and H2, non-need-based aid that is used to meet need should be counted as need-based aid.
78:Note: Suggested order of precedence for counting non-need money as need-based:
89:1. Non-need institutional grants | 283:6. Non-need outside grants
89:2. Non-need tuition waivers | 283:7. Non-need student loans
89:3. Non-need athletic awards | 283:8. Non-need parent loans
89:4. Non-need federal grants | 283:9. Non-need work
89:5. Non-need state grants
78:Non-need-based self-help aid: Loans and jobs from institutional, state, or other sources for which a student need not demonstrate financial need
78:to qualify.
78:Private student loans: A nonfederal loan made by a lender such as a bank, credit union or private lender used to pay for up to the annual cost of
78:education, less any financial aid received.
78:External scholarships and grants: Scholarships and grants received from outside (private) sources that students bring with them (e.g., Kiwanis,
78:National Merit scholarships). The institution may process paperwork to receive the dollars, but it has no role in determining the recipient or the
78:dollar amount awarded.
78:Work study and employment: Federal and state work study aid, and any employment packaged by your institution in financial aid awards.
78:Aid Awarded to Enrolled Undergraduates
78:Enter total dollar amounts awarded to enrolled full-time and less than full-time degree-seeking undergraduates (using the same cohort reported
55:H1
78:in CDS Question $B$1, “total degree-seeking” undergraduates) in the following categories.
78:• | 91:If the data being reported are final figures for the 2024-2025 academic year (see the next item below),

73:Full-time instructional faculty: faculty employed on a full-time basis for instruction (including those with released time for research)
73:Part-time instructional faculty: Adjuncts and other instructors being paid solely for part-time classroom instruction. Also includes full-time
73:faculty teaching less than two semesters, three quarters, two trimesters, or two four-month sessions. Employees who are not
73:considered full-time instruction faculty but who teach one or more non-clinical credit courses may be counted as part-time faculty.
73:Minority faculty: includes faculty who designate themselves as Black, non-Hispanic; American Indian or Alaska Native; Asian, Native
73:Hawaiian or other Pacific Islander, or Hispanic.
73:Doctorate: includes such degrees as Doctor of Philosophy, Doctor of Education, Doctor of Juridical Science, and Doctor of Public
73:Health in any field such as arts, sciences, education, engineering, business, and public administration. Also includes terminal degrees
73:formerly designated as “first professional,” including dentistry (DDS or DMD), medicine (MD), optometry (OD), osteopathic medicine
73:(DO), pharmacy (DPharm or BPharm), podiatric medicine (DPM), veterinary medicine (DVM), chiropractic (DC or DCM), or law (JD).
73:Terminal master’s degree: a master’s degree that is considered the highest degree in a field: example, M. Arch (in architecture) and
73:MFA (master of fine arts in art or theater).

55:I-3. | 73:Undergraduate Class Size
73:In the table below, please use the following definitions to report information about the size of classes and class sections
73:offered in the Fall 2025 term.
73:Class Sections: A class section is an organized course offered for credit, identified by discipline and number,
73:meeting at a stated time or times in a classroom or similar setting, and not a subsection such as a laboratory or
73:discussion session. Undergraduate class sections are defined as any sections in which at least one degree-
73:seeking undergraduate student is enrolled for credit. Exclude distance learning classes and noncredit classes and
73:individual instruction such as dissertation or thesis research, music instruction, or one-to-one readings. Exclude
73:students in independent study, co-operative programs, internships, foreign language taped tutor sessions,
73:practicums, and all students in one-on-one classes. Each class section should be counted only once and should
73:not be duplicated because of course catalog cross-listings.
73:Class Subsections: A class subsection includes any subsection of a course, such as laboratory, recitation, and
73:discussion subsections that are supplementary in nature and are scheduled to meet separately from the lecture
73:portion of the course. Undergraduate subsections are defined as any subsections of courses in which degree-
73:seeking undergraduate students enrolled for credit. As above, exclude noncredit classes and individual
73:instruction such as dissertation or thesis research, music instruction, or one-to-one readings. Each class
73:subsection should be counted only once and should not be duplicated because of cross-listings.
73:Using the above definitions, please report for each of the following class-size intervals the number of class sections and
73:class subsections offered in Fall 2025. For example, a lecture class with 800 students who met at another time in 40
73:separate labs with 20 students should be counted once in the “100+” column in the class section column and 40 times
73:under the “20-29” column of the class subsections table.

55:Associate degree: An award that normally requires at least two but less than four years of full-time equivalent college work.

55:Calendar system: The method by which an institution structures most of its courses for the academic year.

55:*Career and placement services: A range of services, including (often) the following: coordination of visits of employers to campus; aptitude and vocational testing; interest inventories, personal
55:Carnegie units: One year of study or the equivalent in a secondary school subject.
55:Certificate: See Postsecondary award, certificate, or diploma.
55:Class rank: The relative numerical position of a student in his or her graduating class, calculated by the high school on the basis of grade-point average, whether weighted or unweighted.
55:College-preparatory program: Courses in academic subjects (English, history and social studies, foreign languages, mathematics, science, and the arts) that stress preparation for college or univ
55:Common Application: The standard application form distributed by the National Association of Secondary School Principals for a large number of private colleges who are members of the Com
55:*Community service program: Referral center for students wishing to perform volunteer work in the community or participate in volunteer activities coordinated by academic departments.

55:Clock hour: A unit of measure that represents an hour of scheduled instruction given to students. Also referred to as contact hour.
55:Continuous basis (for program enrollment): A calendar system classification that is used by institutions that enroll students at any time during the academic year. For example, a cosmetology sc
55:Cooperative education program: A program that provides for alternate class attendance and employment in business, industry, or government.
55:Cooperative housing: College-owned, -operated, or -affiliated housing in which students share food and housing expenses and participate in household chores to reduce living expenses.
55:*Counseling service: Activities designed to assist students in making plans and decisions related to their education, career, or personal development.
55:Credit: Recognition of attendance or performance in an instructional activity (course or program) that can be applied by a recipient toward the requirements for a degree, diploma, certificate, o
55:Credit course: A course that, if successfully completed, can be applied toward the number of courses required for achieving a degree, diploma, certificate, or other recognized postsecondary cre
55:Credit hour: A unit of measure representing an hour (50 minutes) of instruction over a 15-week period in a semester or trimester system or a 10-week period in a quarter system. It is applied tow
55:Cross-registration: A system whereby students enrolled at one institution may take courses at another institution without having to apply to the second institution.
55:Deferred admission: The practice of permitting admitted students to postpone enrollment, usually for a period of one academic term or one year.
55:Degree: An award conferred by a college, university, or other postsecondary education institution as official recognition for the successful completion of a program of studies.
55:Degree-seeking students: Students enrolled in courses for credit who are recognized by the institution as seeking a degree or recognized postsecondary credential. At the undergraduate level, thi
55:*Developmental services: Instructional courses designed for students deficient in the general competencies necessary for a regular postsecondary curriculum and educational setting.
55:Differs by program (calendar system): A calendar system classification that is used by institutions that have occupational/vocational programs of varying length. These schools may enroll studen
55:Diploma: See Postsecondary award, certificate, or diploma.
55:Distance learning: An option for earning course credit at off-campus locations via cable television, internet, satellite classes, videotapes, correspondence courses, or other means.
55:Doctor’s degree-research/scholarship: A Ph.D. or other doctor's degree that requires advanced work beyond the master’s level, including the preparation and defense of a dissertation based on
55:Doctor’s degree-professional practice: A doctor’s degree that is conferred upon completion of a program providing the knowledge and skills for the recognition, credential, or license required fo
55:Doctor’s degree-other: A doctor’s degree that does not meet the definition of a doctor’s degree - research/scholarship or a doctor’s degree - professional practice.
55:Double major: Program in which students may complete two undergraduate programs of study simultaneously.
55:Dual enrollment: Refers to high school students enrolled in college courses for credit. In accordance with IPEDS, student performance is recorded on a college transcript and postsecondary cred
55:Early action plan: An admission plan that allows students to apply and be notified of an admission decision well in advance of the regular notification dates. If admitted, the candidate is not com
55:Early admission: A policy under which students who have not completed high school are admitted and enroll full time in college, usually after completion of their junior year.
55:Early decision plan: A plan that permits students to apply and be notified of an admission decision (and financial aid offer if applicable) well in advance of the regular notification date. Applican
55:English as a Second Language (ESL): A course of study designed specifically for students whose native language is not English.
55:Exchange student program-domestic: Any arrangement between a student and a college that permits study for a semester or more at another college in the United States without extending the a
55:External degree program: A program of study in which students earn credits toward a degree through independent study, college courses, proficiency examinations, and personal experience. Ex
55:Extracurricular activities (as admission factor): Special consideration in the admissions process given for participation in both school and nonschool-related activities of interest to the college, su
55:First-time student: A student attending any institution for the first time at the level enrolled. Includes students enrolled in the fall term who attended a postsecondary institution for the first time
55:First-time, first-year student: A student attending any institution for the first time at the undergraduate level. Includes students enrolled in the fall term who attended college for the first time in
55:First-year student: A student who has completed less than the equivalent of 1 full year of undergraduate work; that is, less than 30 semester hours (in a 120-hour degree program) or less than 90
55:Food and housing (charges)—on campus: Assume double occupancy in institutional housing and 19 meals per week (or maximum meal plan).
55:Full-time student (undergraduate): A student enrolled for 12 or more semester credits, 12 or more quarter credits, or 24 or more clock hours a week each term.
55:Geographical residence (as admission factor): Special consideration in the admission process given to students from a particular region, state, or country of residence.
55:Grade-point average (academic high school GPA): The sum of grade points a student has earned in secondary school divided by the number of courses taken. The most common system of assign
55:Graduate student: A student who holds a bachelor’s or equivalent, and is taking courses at the post-baccalaureate level.
55:*Health services: Free or low cost on-campus primary and preventive health care available to students.

55:High school diploma or recognized equivalent: A document certifying the successful completion of a prescribed secondary school program of studies, or the attainment of satisfactory scores on t
55:Hispanic or Latino: A person of Mexican, Puerto Rican, Cuban, South or Central American, or other Spanish culture or origin, regardless of race.
55:Honors program: Any special program for very able students offering the opportunity for educational enrichment, independent study, acceleration, or some combination of these.
55:Independent study: Academic work chosen or designed by the student with the approval of the department concerned, under an instructor’s supervision, and usually undertaken outside of the r
55:In-state tuition: The tuition charged by institutions to those students who meet the state’s or institution’s residency requirements.
55:International student: See Nonresident.
55:International student group: Student groups that facilitate cultural dialogue, support a diverse campus, assist international students in acclimation and creating a social network.
55:Internship: Any short-term, supervised work experience usually related to a student’s major field, for which the student earns academic credit. The work can be full- or part-time, on- or off-cam
55:*Learning center: Center offering assistance through tutors, workshops, computer programs, or audiovisual equipment in reading, writing, math, and skills such as taking notes, managing time
55:*Legal services: Free or low cost legal advice for a range of issues (personal and other).
55:Liberal arts/career combination: Program in which a student earns undergraduate degrees in two separate fields, one in a liberal arts major and the other in a professional or specialized major,
55:Living learning community: Residential programs that allow students to interact with students who share common interests. In addition to living together, students may also participate in share
55:Master's degree: An award that requires the successful completion of a program of study of generally one or two full-time equivalent academic years of work beyond the bachelor's degree. Som
55:Minority affiliation (as admission factor): Special consideration in the admission process for members of designated racial/ethnic minority groups.
55:*Minority student center: Center with programs, activities, and/or services intended to enhance the college experience of students of color.
55:Model United Nations: A simulation activity focusing on conflict resolution, globalization, and diplomacy. Assuming roles as foreign ambassadors and “delegates,” students conduct research, en
55:Native Hawaiian or Other Pacific Islander: A person having origins in any of the original peoples of Hawaii, Guam, Samoa, or other Pacific Islands.
55:*New student orientation: Orientation addressing the academic, social, emotional, and intellectual issues involved in beginning college. May be a few hours or a few days in length; at some colleg
55:Nonresident: A person who is not a citizen or national of the United States and who is in this country on a visa or temporary basis and does not have the right to remain indefinitely.
55:*On-campus day care: Licensed day care for students’ children (usually age 3 and up); usually for a fee.
55:Open admission: Admission policy under which virtually all secondary school graduates or students with GED equivalency diplomas are admitted without regard to academic record, test scores
55:Other expenses (costs): Include average costs for clothing, laundry, entertainment, medical (if not a required fee), and furnishings.
55:Out-of-state tuition: The tuition charged by institutions to those students who do not meet the institution’s or state’s residency requirements.
55:Outside a dual enrollment program: High school students who simply enroll in credit courses through your institution, and are treated as regularly enrolled college students.
55:Part-time student (undergraduate): A student enrolled for fewer than 12 credits per semester or quarter, or fewer than 24 clock hours a week each term.
55:Permanent Resident or other eligible non-citizen: A person who is not a citizen or national of the United States and who has been admitted as a legal immigrant for the purpose of obtaining perm
55:*Personal counseling: One-on-one or group counseling with trained professionals for students who want to explore personal, educational, or vocational issues.
55:Post-baccalaureate certificate: An award that requires completion of an organized program of study requiring 18 credit hours beyond the bachelor’s; designed for persons who have completed a
55:Post-master’s certificate: An award that requires completion of an organized program of study of 24 credit hours beyond the master’s degree but does not meet the requirements of academic de
55:Postsecondary award, certificate, or diploma: Includes the following three IPEDS definitions for postsecondary awards, certificates, and diplomas of varying durations and credit/contact/clock h
55:Less Than 1 Academic Year: Requires completion of an organized program of study at the postsecondary level (below the baccalaureate degree) in less than 1 academic year (2 semesters or 3 quarters)
55:At Least 1 But Less Than 2 Academic Years: Requires completion of an organized program of study at the postsecondary level (below the baccalaureate degree) in at least 1 but less than 2 full-time eq
55:At Least 2 But Less Than 4 Academic Years: Requires completion of an organized program of study at the postsecondary level (below the baccalaureate degree) in at least 2 but less than 4 full-time eq
55:Private institution: An educational institution controlled by a private individual(s) or by a nongovernmental agency, usually supported primarily by other than public funds, and operated by oth
55:Private for-profit institution: A private institution in which the individual(s) or agency in control receives compensation, other than wages, rent, or other expenses for the assumption of risk.
55:Private nonprofit institution: A private institution in which the individual(s) or agency in control receives no compensation, other than wages, rent, or other expenses for the assumption of risk.
55:Proprietary institution: See Private for-profit institution.
55:Public institution: An educational institution whose programs and activities are operated by publicly elected or appointed school officials, and which is supported primarily by public funds.
55:Quarter calendar system: A calendar system in which the academic year consists of three sessions called quarters of about 12 weeks each. The range may be from 10 to 15 weeks. There may be a
55:Race/ethnicity: Category used to describe groups to which individuals belong, identify with, or belong in the eyes of the community. The categories do not denote scientific definitions of anthrop
55:Race/ethnicity unknown: Category used to classify students or employees whose race/ethnicity is not known and whom institutions are unable to place in one of the specified racial/ethnic categor
55:Recognized Postsecondary Credential: Includes both Title IV eligible degrees, certificates, and other recognized postsecondary credentials. Any credential that is received after completion of a p
55:Religious affiliation/commitment (as admission factor): Special consideration given in the admission process for affiliation with a certain church or faith/religion, commitment to a religious voca
55:*Religious counseling: One-on-one or group counseling with trained professionals for students who want to explore religious problems or issues.
55:Required fees: Fixed sum charged to students for items not covered by tuition and required of such a large proportion of all students that the student who does NOT pay is the exception. Do not
55:Secondary school record (as admission factor): Information maintained by the secondary school that may include such things as the student’s high school transcript, class rank, GPA, and teache
55:Semester calendar system: A calendar system that consists of two semesters during the academic year with about 16 weeks for each semester of instruction. There may be an additional summer
55:Student-designed major: A program of study based on individual interests, designed with the assistance of an adviser.
55:Study abroad: Any arrangement by which a student completes part of the college program studying in another country. Can be at a campus abroad or through a cooperative agreement with som
55:*Summer session: A summer session is shorter than a regular semester and not considered part of the academic year. It is not the third term of an institution operating on a trimester system or
55:Talent/ability (as admission factor): Special consideration given to students with demonstrated talent/abilities in areas of interest to the institution (e.g., sports, the arts, languages, etc.).
55:Teacher certification program: Program designed to prepare students to meet the requirements for certification as teachers in elementary, middle/junior high, and secondary schools.
55:Transfer applicant: An individual who has fulfilled the institution’s requirements to be considered for admission (including payment or waiving of the application fee, if any) and who has previo
55:Transfer student: A student entering the institution for the first time but known to have previously attended a postsecondary institution at the same level (e.g., undergraduate). The student may
55:Transportation (costs): Assume two round trips to student’s hometown per year for students in institutional housing or daily travel to and from your institution for commuter students.
55:Trimester calendar system: An academic year consisting of 3 terms of about 15 weeks each.
55:Tuition: Amount of money charged to students for instructional services. Tuition may be charged per term, per course, or per credit.
55:*Tutoring: May range from one-on-one tutoring in specific subjects to tutoring in an area such as math, reading, or writing. Most tutors are college students; at some colleges, they are specially
55:Unit: a standard of measurement representing hours of academic instruction (e.g., semester credit, quarter credit, clock hour).
55:Undergraduate: A student enrolled in a four- or five-year bachelor’s degree program, an associate degree program, or a vocational or technical program below the baccalaureate.

55:*Visually impaired: Any person whose sight loss is not correctable and is sufficiently severe as to adversely affect educational performance.

55:Wait list: List of students who meet the admission requirements but will only be offered a place in the class if space becomes available.
55:Weekend college: A program that allows students to take a complete course of study and attend classes only on weekends.
55:White: A person having origins in any of the original peoples of Europe, the Middle East, or North Africa.

220:Financial Aid Definitions
55:External scholarships and grants: Scholarships and grants received from outside (private) sources that students bring with them (e.g., Kiwanis, National Merit scholarships). The institution may
55:Financial aid applicant: Any applicant who submits any one of the institutionally required financial aid applications/forms, such as the FAFSA.
55:Indebtedness: Aggregate dollar amount borrowed through any loan program (federal, state, subsidized, unsubsidized, private, etc.; excluding parent loans) while the student was enrolled at an i
55:Institutional scholarships and grants: Endowed scholarships, annual gifts and tuition funded grants for which the institution determines the recipient.
55:Financial need: As determined by your institution using the federal methodology and/or your institution's own standards.
55:Need-based aid: College-funded or college-administered award from institutional, state, federal, or other sources for which a student must have financial need to qualify. This includes both insti
55:Need-based scholarship or grant aid: Scholarships and grants from institutional, state, federal, or other sources for which a student must have financial need to qualify.
55:Need-based self-help aid: Loans and jobs from institutional, state, federal, or other sources for which a student must demonstrate financial need to qualify.
55:Non-need-based scholarship or grant aid: Scholarships and grants, gifts, or merit-based aid from institutional, state, federal, or other sources (including unrestricted funds or gifts and endowme

55:Non-need-based self-help aid: Loans and jobs from institutional, state, or other sources for which a student need not demonstrate financial need to qualify.
55:Work study and employment: Federal and state work study aid, and any employment packaged by your institution in financial aid awards.
